Mid-air retrieval by helicopter has since been demonstrated on a returning orbital booster, in the [encyclopaedia entry on mid-air retrieval][ref_mid_air_retrieval], and the supersonic parachute work that Mars entry demanded has produced a decelerator literature far deeper than anything available in 1967. **The technique outlived the aerodynamics**, which is not the outcome anyone would have predicted from a programme that lost two vehicles to a main parachute that never left its container and to line cutters fitted without their charges, in [Vitelli and Hallion][research_hallion_1998].

### The Footprint Became a Safety Calculation

Here the inversion is at its sharpest, in [Falsone and Prandini 2017][research_falsone_prandini_2017], [Kim et al 2018][research_kim_2018], [Lee et al 2018][research_lee_2018_2], [Annaloro et al 2020][research_annaloro_2020], [Braun et al 2020][research_braun_2020_2], [Jorgensen and Sharf 2021][research_jorgensen_sharf_2021], [Santos and Sampaio 2021][research_santos_sampaio_2021], [Bohman et al 2024][research_bohman_2024], [Chen et al 2025][research_chen_2025_6], [Chen et al 2025][research_chen_2025_7], [Jain and Hastings 2025][research_jain_hastings_2025], [Navaz and Ntantis 2026][research_navaz_ntantis_2026], [Park et al 2026][research_park_2026], [Yuricich 2026][research_yuricich_2026].

**PRIME wanted a large footprint. Modern practice wants a small and precisely predicted one.** The reachable area treated above as a capability is, for a spent stage or a decaying satellite, a hazard to be bounded, and the same trajectory mathematics now serves debris casualty risk, controlled disposal and range safety. **A quantity computed to show what a vehicle could reach is now computed to prove what it cannot.**

### The Same Relations, on Other Planets

**Nor can the equation tell which planet it is being asked about**, and the cluster below on planetary and superorbital entry, descent and landing is the evidence.

The equilibrium glide with a different gravitational parameter and a different atmosphere is what every planetary entry and every aerocapture study solves. Mars is where the modern work concentrates, because it has enough atmosphere to matter and not enough to make entry easy, and because the crossrange question there is the same question PRIME asked, being whether a vehicle can reach a chosen site rather than wherever the ballistic track puts it.

### How It Flies, Computed to a Standard Nobody Had

Underneath all of it is the aerodynamic and aerothermodynamic prediction that the period could only estimate, surveyed below under hypersonic aerodynamics and under heating.

The decades between PRIME and the current revival were not empty, and 1,411 of the survey's research records were published between 1973 and 2009.

### What the Survey Shows

**The arithmetic has not moved at all.** Crossrange still goes as the square of the lift-to-drag ratio, the optimal bank angle is still 45 degrees, the footprint still goes as the cube, and the once-per-orbit requirement is still about a lift-to-drag ratio of one.

**What moved twice is the requirement.** It went from the Air Force wanting a spacecraft that could come home on the next pass, to nobody wanting that and crewed vehicles giving up ninety-one percent of the capability, to a different community wanting lateral range specifically because it defeats prediction.

**And the thing PRIME was worst at turned out to be the thing that lasted.** The programme lost two of three vehicles to the recovery system. Mid-air retrieval is now a demonstrated commercial technique, in the [encyclopaedia entry on mid-air retrieval][ref_mid_air_retrieval], and the aerodynamic configuration it was carrying is not flown by anyone.

### Parachutes, decelerators and recovery

**The recovery system and its descendants**, being parachutes and their deployment, inflation, reefing and fabrics, ballutes and inflatable or deployable decelerators, and the mid-air retrieval of a descending payload. It is the largest cluster, and it includes parachute engineering for aircraft, ordnance and personnel because they share the deployment and inflation problem. 135 of the 650 titles say supersonic and 161 name a ballute, an aeroshell or an inflatable or deployable decelerator.

### Hypersonic aerodynamics, flow fields and the boundary layer

**The flow the vehicle flies through**, being blunt-body and slender-body hypersonic aerodynamics, nonequilibrium and rarefied flow, the boundary layer and its transition, wakes, the plasma sheath and communications blackout, and the computational test cases of the double ellipse and the double ellipsoid.

### Entry guidance, trajectory optimisation and hypersonic flight control

**Steering an entry vehicle and computing the trajectory it should fly**, including predictor-corrector guidance, numerical trajectory optimisation and the attitude control of hypersonic vehicles. **277 of the 412 records date from 2010 or later.** 85 titles name a convex, pseudospectral, predictor-corrector, evolutionary, swarm, neural or learning method, which is what the free computation described above looks like in a title.

### Hypersonic glide vehicles, their tracking and their interception

**The glide vehicle as a weapon**, with its trajectory design and control, and the tracking, prediction and interception of it. **235 of the 252 records date from 2010 or later**, and 91 of the titles name tracking, prediction, estimation, detection, radar, interception, intention or defence, which is the inversion the analytic subsection above describes, from designing a manoeuvring entry body to predicting one.

### The Space Shuttle orbiter and its requirement

**The vehicle that PRIME's crossrange fed**, being the orbiter's entry, guidance, aerothermodynamics, thermal protection, flight control and landing. **162 of the 218 records date from before 1990**, which is the shape of a literature about a vehicle that was designed in the 1970s and flew its entries from 1981.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**Treating PRIME through crossrange alone gets the programme wrong in at least three ways.**

**The vehicle was not only a manoeuvring experiment.** It carried a heat shield the programme wanted measured, and ASSET had already been devoted to structures and heating. The sources disagree on how far PRIME was a heating experiment. The official history lists the acquisition of heat shield and aerodynamic data first among the five flight objectives, in [Vitelli and Hallion][research_hallion_1998], while [Heppenheimer][ref_heppenheimer_1999] says that PRIME did not attempt to produce heating data because ASSET had covered it. Heating and manoeuvring were both objectives and the article's keystone selects one. The wider class of entry vehicles, most of which never manoeuvred at all, fills the survey's cluster on capsules and ballistic entry, and the field it sits inside is the survey as a whole.

**The equilibrium glide is not what it flew.** The model assumes a smooth glide that begins below circular speed. PRIME entered at 2.03 degrees from 400,000 feet, faster than the circular speed the model uses, and the programme's computed trajectory dips and oscillates rather than gliding in equilibrium, so it reached the crossrange it did under conditions the model describes only approximately, in [Vitelli and Hallion][research_hallion_1998]. That the numbers agree to one percent at the lower disputed lift-to-drag ratio is encouraging and is not proof.

**The reference area is derived, not published.** Everything in the thermal section inherits that. The crossrange result does not, and for that reason the crossrange is the keystone and the heating is not.

A fourth concerns the article's framing. **A vehicle whose recovery system failed twice in three flights is a programme about recovery at least as much as about entry**, and it gets one short section here.

## The Source Base

**The research survey holds 4,642 research records drawn from the Technical Reports Server of the National Aeronautics and Space Administration, NASA, from the Defense Technical Information Center and from the Crossref index**, of which 585 link to the NASA Technical Reports Server, 281 are Defense Technical Information Center reports by host or by that center's identifier prefix, and the remaining 3,776 are other records reached through Crossref. The report servers therefore hold 866 records, being 18.7 percent of the survey, and its median publication year is 2009.

**The subject has a deep period record.** Of the 4,554 research records that carry a year, 1,056, or 23.2 percent, predate 1976, which reflects that lifting entry was worked out in a concentrated burst between about 1958 and 1972 and then largely left alone until the current revival. **The period record names the subject in its own vocabulary.** Where the modern literature says crossrange, the period reports say lateral range, roll modulation and maneuvering range, and the period work is found under those words.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the NASA Technical Reports Server or on the Defense Technical Information Center's own host, or when it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. **The records this article's own argument rests on are report primaries under that rule**, among them the project's official history in [Vitelli and Hallion][research_hallion_1998], [Reed's history of the lifting bodies][research_r_dale_reed_1997], the X-24A data in [Cochrane and Graham][research_john_cochrane_kenneth_graham_1971] and [McTigue][research_john_g_mctigue_1970], and the planform figures in [Saltzman, Wang and Iliff][research_saltzman_2002]. **The rule does not reach the journal papers of the period**, such as the lateral range analyses published by the American Institute of Aeronautics and Astronautics, so 18.7 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is first-hand.

### Thin Subjects

**Five subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term from each group.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| the vehicle itself, by its own names | SV-5D, SV-5, X-23, X-23A, Project PRIME, PRIME vehicle, program, programme, reentry or re-entry | 3 |
| the bank angle that maximises crossrange | crossrange, cross range, lateral range together with bank, banking, roll, rolling | 2 |
| mid-air retrieval of a returning payload | mid air, midair, aerial recovery, aerial retrieval, in air capturing, in air capture, air snatch | 20 |
| the once-around recall requirement | once around, abort once, landing site access, recall, return to launch site, global landing | 2 |
| the effective heat of ablation | heat of ablation, heats of ablation, ablation heat | 0 |

**The vehicle itself is almost absent from the titles.** Three titles name it by SV-5, X-23 or PRIME. One is the project's official history in [Vitelli and Hallion][research_hallion_1998], one defines the entry environment of an assured crew return vehicle derived from the X-23 and X-24A in [Campbell et al 1996][research_campbell_caram_1996], and the third, of 2023, gives the designation X-23 to a hypersonic glide vehicle in its title, in [Butler et al 2023][research_butler_benitez_2023]. The flight record this article reconstructs therefore rests on the official history and on Reed rather than on a technical literature of the vehicle.

**The bank angle that maximises crossrange has two titles, both of the period**, lateral range control by banking in [Baradell 1962][research_baradell_1962] and roll modulation for maximum lateral range in [Wagner 1965][research_wagner_1965]. The optimum of 45 degrees derived above is standard and is rarely the subject of a title.

**Mid-air retrieval has a small literature of its own.** Of the 20 titles, 5 concern returning boosters or reusable launch vehicles, and [Catch a Falling Star 1993][research_catch_a_1993] records the parachute lessons of the Air Force space capsule mid-air recovery programme from 1959 to 1985, the technique this article records as already in service for returning film capsules when PRIME flew.

**The recall requirement that motivated the vehicle has two titles**, abort-once-around dispersion in [Kyle 1975][research_kyle_1975] and global landing site access by skip trajectories in [Bryant 2002][research_bryant_2002]. The requirement's history is in [Heppenheimer][ref_heppenheimer_1999] rather than in a research literature.

**No title names the effective heat of ablation**, so the cross-check above that recovers 27.8 megajoules per kilogram from a published thickness stands on the relation shown rather than on a measured value in the survey.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject.** The programme acronyms do not identify its literature. PRIME collides with prime numbers and prime movers, and START with the Strategic Arms Reduction Treaty and with the ordinary English verb, so neither word admits a record to the survey. Beyond the acronyms, footprint belongs to carbon, water and ecological accounting and to tyre contact, recovery to economics and to waste heat, ablation to medicine, to laser materials processing and to the ablators of inertial fusion targets, charring to fire science, and entry to almost everything. **Re-entry is also the return of prisoners to the community**, the subject of a large criminal justice literature, and a term of cardiac electrophysiology and of software, and parachute also names a golden parachute in finance, a heart valve malformation, a mine ventilation stopping and a game for schoolchildren.

**Several further families share this subject's words and nothing else.** Lateral motion of a vehicle is a term of art in railway dynamics and in road-vehicle lane keeping. Maneuvering range names an instrumented air combat facility off the East Coast, so the phrase also titles a construction quality assurance plan. Lateral range is also a term in search and detection theory. Glider, maneuvering and hovering all belong to underwater vehicles as well as to aircraft. **Guidance, landing and control are shared with tactical missiles, small unmanned aircraft, rotorcraft and spacecraft operating in orbit**, and a reading of this subject's titles removes records of all four. Impact-angle and homing guidance of tactical missiles, the landing and flight control of drones and multirotors, helicopter flight test, and rendezvous, formation flight and debris impact on orbit are excluded, because none of them concerns a vehicle returning through the atmosphere.

**Records that share a word with the subject and nothing else are excluded.** Beyond the families above, the excluded classes include road, rail and ground transport, clinical medicine and biology, ion, surface and plasma physics, robotics and manufacturing, building heating and energy systems, combustion, fire science, boiling, critical heat flux and industrial heat exchange, thermal analysis as a laboratory chemistry discipline, atmospheric remote sensing and radio propagation, boundary-layer meteorology, airline operations, generic fluid mechanics and numerical mathematics, satellite communications, and electronics and materials. Records that are not works are excluded as well, meaning figures, tables, supplementary files, peer-review reports, notices of erratum, correction, retraction or withdrawal, and journal front matter such as reviewer lists and indexes, because a survey counts research works and those are parts of works or editorial events rather than works. A review of a book, a reference or definition entry, a notice such as a news item, a press release or an editorial preface, and a second deposit of a document the survey already holds, such as a report server copy or a reprint of a journal article, are not admitted either, because none of them is a further research work.

**Some records of doubtful relevance are kept**, because they bear on entry, flight or the design of a vehicle like this one without being about it. They are spacecraft attitude control by reaction jets, wheels and gyroscopes, crew physiology during entry and landing, the entry heating of meteoroids and micrometeorites, lunar and planetary landing and the erosion a landing rocket causes, aircraft structural weight estimation, rocket engine components, the propulsive landing of returning boosters, and the air-breathing hypersonic vehicle control literature, whose vehicles fly the same speed range without returning from orbit. **Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only.

## Epistemic State

**Historical fact, as reported by the accessible record.** The SV-5D PRIME was built by Martin Marietta for the United States Air Force under the START programme. Four were built, three were launched on SLV-3 Atlas boosters from Vandenberg on 21 December 1966, 5 March 1967 and 19 April 1967, and the fourth flight was cancelled on 12 May 1967. The first two were lost to recovery-system failures and the third was recovered in mid-air by a JC-130B at 12,000 feet. The crossranges flown were zero, 654 miles and more than 710 miles in the official history's narrative, taken here as 1,053 and 1,143 kilometres, while its summary table gives 499 and more than 710 nautical miles. The second flight held a constant bank of 46 degrees until terminal guidance. The third re-entered at 400,000 feet, 26,808 feet per second and 2.03 degrees, 1,360 nautical miles from launch. Martin's design figure for the hypersonic lift-to-drag ratio was 1.2 or better. All of this is in [Vitelli and Hallion][research_hallion_1998]. The vehicle was 6 feet 8 inches long, and its recorded weights run from about 800 to 895 pounds, with 894 used here. The surviving vehicle is at the National Museum of the United States Air Force.

**Historical fact, on the designation.** The nomenclature record shows a request for X-23A submitted on 16 November 1965 for the SV-5P, disapproved on 15 December 1965 on the stated ground that the aircraft was unmanned, and a late-1966 decision to seek X-23A for the SV-5D and X-24A for the SV-5P, of which only the second was ever submitted, in the [standard reference on designations][ref_designation_missing]. **This article reports that record and does not adjudicate between it and universal usage.**

**Engineering analysis, performed here and checkable.** The equilibrium-glide crossrange relation, its closed form, the optimal bank angle of 45 degrees, the required crossrange of half the ground-track spacing, and the resulting lift-to-drag requirement of 0.978 by the closed form and 1.018 by exact integration, are all derived in this article from stated assumptions. The simultaneous solution of downrange and crossrange for the bank angle and lift-to-drag ratio, and the inversion of crossrange alone at a documented bank angle, are likewise derived here. The heating figures follow from the Sutton and Graves correlation applied to the equilibrium-glide density.

**Inference, and flagged as such.** That the vehicle's effective lift-to-drag ratio through the manoeuvring phase was about 1.01 on the second flight and 1.05 on the third is an inference from the documented crossranges at the documented bank angles under the equilibrium-glide model. It rises to about 1.14 if the summary table's nautical miles are right, and it lies between the programme's trim curve at the flown angle of attack and that curve's maximum, below Martin's design figure of 1.2. The simultaneous solution of downrange and crossrange is not used as a measurement, because at the documented boost range it demands a bank angle the record contradicts. That nobody submitted the X-23A request because the 1965 refusal had said unmanned vehicles need none is a reading offered by the designation reference and is not documented.

**Assumptions that everything downstream inherits.** The reference area of 1.386 square metres is scaled from the X-24A and is not published for this vehicle. The entry speed of 25,200 feet per second, the nose radius of 0.1 metres, the hypersonic drag coefficient of unity, the ablator density of 550 kilograms per cubic metre, the parachute drag coefficient of 0.75, and the nominal lift-to-drag ratios of the capsules and the Space Shuttle orbiter in the comparison table are all assumed. The record's own speeds run from 21,853 to 26,808 feet per second.

**What the record does not settle.** The vehicle's lift-to-drag ratio in flight, beyond what the programme's trim curve shows. Its actual reference area. Whether the crossranges are statute or nautical miles. The bank angle flown on the third flight. The stagnation heat load, which the programme's stated figure and its computed heating curve give very differently. The peak deceleration and heating as measured, which were certainly telemetered and are not in the accessible literature. And the reason the X-23A request was never sent.

## Out of Scope

The ASSET phase of the START programme, which deserves its own treatment. The Atlas SLV-3 as a launch vehicle. The X-24A and X-24B, which follow in this series. The mid-air retrieval technique and its origins in satellite film return. The detailed chemistry of charring ablators. The Space Shuttle crossrange requirement's full political history, of which only the arithmetic is treated here.

## Conclusion

**PRIME asked whether a spacecraft could steer on the way home, and the answer was yes, by about a thousand kilometres.**

The number matters more than it looks. A returning vehicle needs roughly half a ground-track spacing of crossrange to reach a chosen site without waiting, and at the equator that requires a lift-to-drag ratio of 0.978 by the relation the period used and 1.018 when the approximation in that relation is removed. **A lifting body near unity is therefore not a modest capability but the threshold one**, and the difference between it and a ballistic capsule is not a matter of degree. One can come home when it chooses and the other cannot.

One more measure shows how steeply that capability is bought. The reachable ground area scales as the crossrange half-width times the downrange depth, and those go as the square and the first power of the lift-to-drag ratio respectively, so

$$A_\text{footprint} \propto \left(\frac{L}{D}\right)^3$$

**The footprint goes as the cube.** Raising the ratio from 1.0 to 1.2 enlarges the region a vehicle can reach by a factor of 1.73. Twenty percent of lift-to-drag ratio was therefore worth arguing about, and the disagreement in the sources matters.

The vehicle that demonstrated this weighed 894 pounds, flew three times, was lost twice on the way back, and carries a designation that was never requested for it. **All four of those facts deserve to be kept.**
